### CLEAR-KGQA: Clarification-Enhanced Ambiguity Resolution for Knowledge Graph Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09665v1)
- **Authors**: Liqiang Wen, Guanming Xiong, Tong Mo, Bing Li, Weiping Li, Wen Zhao
- **Abstract**: This study addresses the challenge of ambiguity in knowledge graph question answering (KGQA). While recent KGQA systems have made significant progress, particularly with the integration of large language models (LLMs), they typically assume user queries are unambiguous, which is an assumption that rarely holds in real-world applications. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework that dynamically handles both entity ambiguity (e.g., distinguishing between entities with similar names) and intent ambiguity (e.g., clarifying different interpretations of user queries) through interactive clarification. Our approach employs a Bayesian inference mechanism to quantify query ambiguity and guide LLMs in determining when and how to request clarification from users within a multi-turn dialogue framework. We further develop a two-agent interaction framework where an LLM-based user simulator enables iterative refinement of logical forms through simulated user feedback. Experimental results on the WebQSP and CWQ dataset demonstrate that our method significantly improves performance by effectively resolving semantic ambiguities. Additionally, we contribute a refined dataset of disambiguated queries, derived from interaction histories, to facilitate future research in this direction.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the CLEAR-KGQA paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CLEAR-KGQA, a framework to address ambiguity in Knowledge Graph Question Answering (KGQA) systems. It tackles both entity ambiguity (multiple entities with similar names) and intent ambiguity (multiple interpretations of the user's question) through interactive clarification. The system uses a Bayesian inference mechanism to quantify ambiguity, guiding an LLM to decide when and how to ask clarification questions. The framework includes a new "AskForClarification" tool, a two-agent interaction setting with an LLM-based user simulator, and a refined dataset of disambiguated queries created from interaction histories. The paper reports performance improvements on WebQSP and CWQ datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach in resolving ambiguities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by directly tackling the often-overlooked problem of ambiguity in KGQA. While previous KGQA systems often assume well-formed and unambiguous queries, CLEAR-KGQA actively recognizes and resolves ambiguity, making it more realistic for real-world applications. Specifically:

*   **Novel Integration of Bayesian Inference:** The paper's use of Bayesian inference to quantify ambiguity is a clever way to guide the clarification process. The plug-and-play nature of this ambiguity calculation is a strength.
*   **Introduction of the AskForClarification Tool:** Adding a dedicated tool for clarification is a logical extension of the "tool-based" approach in recent LLM-based KGQA.
*   **Two-Agent Interaction Framework:** The idea of using an LLM-based user simulator for iterative refinement of logical forms is a solid approach for both training and evaluation, especially given the difficulty of obtaining real user interaction data.
*   **Dataset Creation:** Creating a disambiguated dataset from interaction histories addresses a crucial need in this area. The release of this dataset will likely facilitate further research.

However, it's important to note some limitations to the paper's novelty:

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The paper builds upon the existing "tool-based" LLM agent framework in KGQA, specifically the Interactive-KBQA framework. While it introduces significant improvements, it doesn't represent a complete paradigm shift.
*   **Established Concepts:** The use of Bayesian inference and dialogue systems for ambiguity resolution are not new in general, but their application in KGQA within an LLM agent framework is a more significant contribution.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the robustness and user-friendliness of KGQA systems. By effectively handling ambiguity, CLEAR-KGQA can provide more accurate and relevant answers in real-world scenarios. The demonstrated performance improvements on WebQSP (which contains queries from real users) and CWQ provide solid evidence of the approach's effectiveness. The release of the disambiguated dataset is also a valuable contribution that could stimulate further work in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly defined problem and approach:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of ambiguity in KGQA and provides a well-defined solution framework.
*   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The paper includes ablation studies and comparisons with strong baselines, providing evidence of the effectiveness of each component of the framework.
*   **Practical contribution:** The approach is practical and can be integrated into existing KGQA systems. The dataset release also enhances practical utility.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper conducts thorough error analysis.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on LLMs:** The approach relies heavily on the capabilities of LLMs. Performance could be affected by the specific LLM used and its ability to understand and respond to clarification requests.
*   **Limited User Simulation Complexity:** The LLM-based user simulator, while useful, may not fully capture the complexities of real user behavior, especially in multi-turn interactions.
*   **Clarification Strategy:** While the Bayesian approach helps guide clarification, the paper could explore more sophisticated strategies for formulating clarification questions to maximize user engagement and minimize interaction turns.
*   **Lack of qualitative analysis:** Including example dialogues would have strengthened the paper and further demonstrated how different types of ambiguities are being handled by the proposed approach.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper's clear and well-defined framework, coupled with the release of a disambiguated dataset, is likely to influence future research in KGQA. It provides a solid foundation for further exploration of ambiguity resolution techniques, including more sophisticated clarification strategies and user simulation methods.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the KGQA field by tackling the practical problem of ambiguity resolution. The integration of Bayesian inference and the design of the AskForClarification tool are novel and effective, as demonstrated by the improved performance on benchmark datasets. The creation and release of the disambiguated dataset will also benefit future research.

However, the paper does not represent a groundbreaking paradigm shift. It builds upon existing LLM-based KGQA architectures and uses established techniques for ambiguity resolution. The reliance on LLMs and the limitations of the user simulator are also weaknesses. While the performance improvements are substantial, the dependence on potentially expensive LLM calls raises questions about scalability. The potential complexity of real user interactions compared to the simulated user interaction also slightly reduces the impact on real world applications. Still, on balance, the paper's contribution is significant and warrants a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can LLMs Revolutionize the Design of Explainable and Efficient TinyML Models?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09685v1)
- **Authors**: Christophe El Zeinaty, Wassim Hamidouche, Glenn Herrou, Daniel Menard, Merouane Debbah
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces a novel framework for designing efficient neural network architectures specifically tailored to tiny machine learning (TinyML) platforms. By leveraging large language models (LLMs) for neural architecture search (NAS), a vision transformer (ViT)-based knowledge distillation (KD) strategy, and an explainability module, the approach strikes an optimal balance between accuracy, computational efficiency, and memory usage. The LLM-guided search explores a hierarchical search space, refining candidate architectures through Pareto optimization based on accuracy, multiply-accumulate operations (MACs), and memory metrics. The best-performing architectures are further fine-tuned using logits-based KD with a pre-trained ViT-B/16 model, which enhances generalization without increasing model size. Evaluated on the CIFAR-100 dataset and deployed on an STM32H7 microcontroller (MCU), the three proposed models, LMaNet-Elite, LMaNet-Core, and QwNet-Core, achieve accuracy scores of 74.50%, 74.20% and 73.00%, respectively. All three models surpass current state-of-the-art (SOTA) models, such as MCUNet-in3/in4 (69.62% / 72.86%) and XiNet (72.27%), while maintaining a low computational cost of less than 100 million MACs and adhering to the stringent 320 KB static random-access memory (SRAM) constraint. These results demonstrate the efficiency and performance of the proposed framework for TinyML platforms, underscoring the potential of combining LLM-driven search, Pareto optimization, KD, and explainability to develop accurate, efficient, and interpretable models. This approach opens new possibilities in NAS, enabling the design of efficient architectures specifically suited for TinyML.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and will provide a summary and a critical evaluation as requested.

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) for Neural Architecture Search (NAS) specifically tailored for Tiny Machine Learning (TinyML) platforms.  It combines LLM-driven search with Pareto optimization based on accuracy, multiply-accumulate operations (MACs), and memory constraints.  The approach incorporates Knowledge Distillation (KD) using a pre-trained Vision Transformer (ViT) to enhance generalization without increasing model size.  Furthermore, the framework explores the use of LLMs to provide explanations for the architecture design choices, aiming for explainable AI (XAI).  The proposed models (LMaNet-Elite, LMaNet-Core, and QwNet-Core) demonstrate state-of-the-art accuracy on the CIFAR-100 dataset while adhering to stringent memory and computational resource limitations, especially within the constraints of the STM32H7 microcontroller. The authors make their framework and models publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The combination of LLM-guided NAS, Pareto optimization *and* explainability specifically for TinyML is a relatively novel contribution. While LLMs have been used for NAS previously, this paper focuses on deployment-specific constraints and, importantly, incorporates XAI.  The exploration of explainable AI aspects in NAS is a strong point. The combination of these elements is novel.
*   **Weakness:** The individual components (LLM-NAS, Pareto optimization, Knowledge Distillation) are not entirely new on their own. The novelty primarily stems from the *integration* of these existing methods within the context of TinyML and with an XAI component.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** The work addresses a significant and growing need in the TinyML space: how to automate the design of efficient and accurate models within extremely tight resource constraints. Achieving SOTA performance on CIFAR-100 while staying within the STM32H7's limitations is practically relevant. The inclusion of XAI opens avenues for greater trust and adoption of these automated methods. Releasing their framework further allows researchers to test/extend the framework.
*   **Weakness:** While CIFAR-100 is a widely used benchmark, it can be argued that it's not fully representative of the diverse applications in TinyML (e.g., sensor data analysis, voice recognition). It will be important to see this framework applied on real-world datasets to see its effect. Also, although the XAI component sounds nice, the XAI appears to merely be LLM responses that justify the choices made in designing the architecture. More rigorious or formal methods for XAI may be more appropriate to improve the reliability and robustness of the XAI.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

Here's my rationale:

*   **Positive Aspects (Adding to the score):**
    *   The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in TinyML.
    *   The combination of LLM-NAS, Pareto optimization, KD, and XAI is a novel integration of components, specifically targeted at deployment-specific constraints of TinyML.
    *   The results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on a common benchmark.
    *   The inclusion of an explainability component is a valuable addition.
    *   The authors release their code/framework.

*   **Negative Aspects (Subtracting from the score):**
    *   The individual components are not ground-breakingly novel.
    *   The evaluation is limited to CIFAR-100 and STM32H7, lacking real-world application experiments.
    *   The XAI component is not rigorous.

The score reflects the recognition of the paper's novel integration of existing techniques to address a significant problem, achieving impressive results. However, the limitations in evaluation and the somewhat limited scope of the XAI component prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper is a valuable contribution that pushes the boundaries of what's possible in automated TinyML model design and takes a good first step in explainable AI for NAS. The usefulness of the framework for diverse applications in the real world, and a more robust XAI integration, warrant further exploration.

### SPICE: A Synergistic, Precise, Iterative, and Customizable Image Editing Workflow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09697v1)
- **Authors**: Kenan Tang, Yanhong Li, Yao Qin
- **Abstract**: Recent prompt-based image editing models have demonstrated impressive prompt-following capability at structural editing tasks. However, existing models still fail to perform local edits, follow detailed editing prompts, or maintain global image quality beyond a single editing step. To address these challenges, we introduce SPICE, a training-free workflow that accepts arbitrary resolutions and aspect ratios, accurately follows user requirements, and improves image quality consistently during more than 100 editing steps. By synergizing the strengths of a base diffusion model and a Canny edge ControlNet model, SPICE robustly handles free-form editing instructions from the user. SPICE outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on a challenging realistic image-editing dataset consisting of semantic editing (object addition, removal, replacement, and background change), stylistic editing (texture changes), and structural editing (action change) tasks. Not only does SPICE achieve the highest quantitative performance according to standard evaluation metrics, but it is also consistently preferred by users over existing image-editing methods. We release the workflow implementation for popular diffusion model Web UIs to support further research and artistic exploration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SPICE paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPICE, a training-free workflow designed to enhance prompt-based image editing.  SPICE addresses limitations of existing models, such as difficulty with local edits, following detailed prompts, and maintaining global image quality across multiple editing steps. It synergizes a base diffusion model with a Canny edge ControlNet. The workflow involves three key steps: mask generation (with context dots and soft inpainting), color and edge hint generation, and two-stage denoising. SPICE aims for precise, iterative, and customizable image editing, achieving superior quantitative performance on realistic image editing datasets (EditEval) and demonstrating user preference over existing methods. The paper emphasizes ease of integration into existing diffusion model Web UIs and low computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *specific combination* of established techniques (diffusion models, ControlNets, masking, and hints) into a coherent, training-free *workflow*, rather than in introducing fundamentally new algorithms. The "context dots" and the structured three-step process for iterative editing are the most innovative aspects.

*   **Significance:** The significance of SPICE is potentially high due to its accessibility and practical benefits. Its key strengths are:

    *   *Training-free*: avoids the expensive and cumbersome process of model training.
    *   *Iterative Refinement*: Addresses the problem of image degradation inherent in multi-step editing with other techniques.
    *   *Customization:* It enables fine-grained control over editing through visual hints and hyperparameter adjustments, going beyond textual prompts.
    *   *Easy integration:* Facilitating its adoption within existing diffusion model ecosystems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides thorough quantitative and qualitative evaluations. It demonstrates superior performance on a challenging dataset (EditEval) and user preference through human studies.
    *   The ablation studies are crucial for understanding the contribution of each component of the workflow.
    *   The paper addresses a practical problem (iterative image editing) with a tangible solution that can be readily used by researchers and artists.
    *   The paper acknowledges and attempts to solve one of the biggest issues with current image generation techniques: the inability of models to do precisely what the user wants.
    *   The extensive supplementary materials provide ample information for replication and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components are well-established. The *incremental* nature of the contribution needs to be acknowledged.
    *   The emphasis on a training-free approach may limit the potential for further performance gains achievable through task-specific training.
    *   Some of the described benefits depend heavily on the capabilities of the underlying diffusion model (FLUX.1). Generalizability across different diffusion model architectures requires further validation.
    *   The limitations section is honest but points to potential areas of further improvement in texture blending and handling diverse image sources.
    *   While a comprehensive evaluation is done, the results are primarily on an existing benchmark, EditEval. SPICE would benefit from evaluation on specific applications, such as photo editing or creative design, where its strengths are more directly utilized.

*   **Potential Influence:** SPICE has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Providing a practical, high-quality alternative to training specialized image editing models.
    *   Encouraging further research into synergistic workflows that combine different established components for improved image manipulation capabilities.
    *   Serving as a baseline for evaluating future prompt-based image editing methods.

**Overall:**

SPICE is a well-executed workflow that addresses important challenges in prompt-based image editing. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the synergistic combination, the training-free nature, and the ability to iteratively refine edits make it a valuable contribution. Its accessible design and practical advantages position it to be widely adopted and influential. While the influence on the field could be limited due to the individual components being well established, the workflow as a whole represents an impactful improvement.

### DUMP: Automated Distribution-Level Curriculum Learning for RL-based LLM Post-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09710v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenting Wang, Guofeng Cui, Kun Wan, Wentian Zhao
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in reinforcement learning (RL)-based post-training have led to notable improvements in large language models (LLMs), particularly in enhancing their reasoning capabilities to handle complex tasks. However, most existing methods treat the training data as a unified whole, overlooking the fact that modern LLM training often involves a mixture of data from diverse distributions-varying in both source and difficulty. This heterogeneity introduces a key challenge: how to adaptively schedule training across distributions to optimize learning efficiency. In this paper, we present a principled curriculum learning framework grounded in the notion of distribution-level learnability. Our core insight is that the magnitude of policy advantages reflects how much a model can still benefit from further training on a given distribution. Based on this, we propose a distribution-level curriculum learning framework for RL-based LLM post-training, which leverages the Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) principle to dynamically adjust sampling probabilities for different distrubutions. This approach prioritizes distributions with either high average advantage (exploitation) or low sample count (exploration), yielding an adaptive and theoretically grounded training schedule. We instantiate our curriculum learning framework with GRPO as the underlying RL algorithm and demonstrate its effectiveness on logic reasoning datasets with multiple difficulties and sources. Our experiments show that our framework significantly improves convergence speed and final performance, highlighting the value of distribution-aware curriculum strategies in LLM post-training. Code: https://github.com/ZhentingWang/DUMP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DUMP: Automated Distribution-Level Curriculum Learning for RL-based LLM Post-training" addresses the challenge of training large language models (LLMs) with reinforcement learning (RL) when the training data comes from diverse distributions of varying difficulty.  It proposes DUMP, a novel distribution-level curriculum learning framework that uses the magnitude of policy advantages as a proxy for the learnability of each distribution. DUMP leverages the Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) principle to dynamically adjust the sampling probabilities of different distributions, prioritizing those with high average advantage (exploitation) or low sample count (exploration). The authors instantiate DUMP with the GRPO RL algorithm and demonstrate its effectiveness on logic reasoning datasets, showing improved convergence speed and performance compared to uniform sampling. The paper also includes a theoretical analysis to support the use of absolute advantages as a proxy for learnability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using policy advantages to guide distribution-level curriculum learning is relatively novel in the context of LLM post-training with RL. While curriculum learning and RL are established fields, their combination at the distribution-level with a specific focus on LLMs represents a valuable contribution. Existing work often relies on instance-level difficulty or static, hand-designed curricula. The UCB-based approach for balancing exploitation and exploration further enhances the novelty of the method.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of RL-based LLM post-training. By dynamically adapting the training schedule to focus on the most learnable distributions, DUMP can accelerate convergence, leading to better performance and reduced training costs. This is particularly relevant as RL becomes increasingly important for enhancing LLMs' capabilities in reasoning and other complex tasks. The paper also highlights an important underexplored aspect of RL-based LLM post-training.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled approach:** DUMP is grounded in the notion of distribution-level learnability and formalized using the UCB principle. This provides a theoretical foundation for the method.
    *   **Simplicity:** The algorithm is relatively simple to implement and integrate into existing RL pipelines.
    *   **Empirical results:** The experiments on logic reasoning datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of DUMP in improving convergence speed and performance.
    *   **Theoretical analysis:** The paper includes a theoretical analysis that supports the use of absolute advantages as a surrogate for distribution-level learnability.
    *   **Clear writing and presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited evaluation:** The evaluation is limited to logic reasoning datasets. It would be beneficial to evaluate DUMP on other types of tasks and datasets to demonstrate its generalizability.
    *   **Reliance on GRPO:** While the authors mention that DUMP is compatible with any advantage-based RL algorithm, the experiments are only conducted with GRPO.
    *   **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** The performance of DUMP may be sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters, such as the window size k and the temperature τ. Although they discuss these parameters, a more comprehensive analysis of hyperparameter tuning would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Stationarity assumption:** The theoretical analysis relies on the assumption that the true expected advantage remains approximately stationary over a local training window. This assumption may not always hold in practice.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RL-based LLM post-training by highlighting the importance of distribution-aware curriculum learning. The DUMP framework provides a practical and theoretically grounded approach to address this challenge.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper demonstrates a novel and principled approach to distribution-level curriculum learning for RL-based LLM post-training, the limited evaluation and potential hyperparameter sensitivity prevent it from achieving a higher score. The theoretical analysis, while valuable, relies on assumptions that may not always hold. It introduces DUMP to address the challenge and present evidence of it.
However, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a practical framework and demonstrating its effectiveness on logic reasoning datasets.

### Can LLM feedback enhance review quality? A randomized study of 20K reviews at ICLR 2025
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09737v1)
- **Authors**: Nitya Thakkar, Mert Yuksekgonul, Jake Silberg, Animesh Garg, Nanyun Peng, Fei Sha, Rose Yu, Carl Vondrick, James Zou
- **Abstract**: Peer review at AI conferences is stressed by rapidly rising submission volumes, leading to deteriorating review quality and increased author dissatisfaction. To address these issues, we developed Review Feedback Agent, a system leveraging multiple large language models (LLMs) to improve review clarity and actionability by providing automated feedback on vague comments, content misunderstandings, and unprofessional remarks to reviewers. Implemented at ICLR 2025 as a large randomized control study, our system provided optional feedback to more than 20,000 randomly selected reviews. To ensure high-quality feedback for reviewers at this scale, we also developed a suite of automated reliability tests powered by LLMs that acted as guardrails to ensure feedback quality, with feedback only being sent to reviewers if it passed all the tests. The results show that 27% of reviewers who received feedback updated their reviews, and over 12,000 feedback suggestions from the agent were incorporated by those reviewers. This suggests that many reviewers found the AI-generated feedback sufficiently helpful to merit updating their reviews. Incorporating AI feedback led to significantly longer reviews (an average increase of 80 words among those who updated after receiving feedback) and more informative reviews, as evaluated by blinded researchers. Moreover, reviewers who were selected to receive AI feedback were also more engaged during paper rebuttals, as seen in longer author-reviewer discussions. This work demonstrates that carefully designed LLM-generated review feedback can enhance peer review quality by making reviews more specific and actionable while increasing engagement between reviewers and authors. The Review Feedback Agent is publicly available at https://github.com/zou-group/review_feedback_agent.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve the quality of peer reviews in AI conferences. It introduces Review Feedback Agent, a system that uses LLMs to provide automated feedback to reviewers on issues like vague comments, content misunderstandings, and unprofessional remarks. Implemented as a randomized control trial at ICLR 2025 with over 20,000 reviews, the results indicate that reviewers who received feedback were more likely to update their reviews, incorporated feedback suggestions, wrote longer and more informative reviews, and engaged more actively in author-reviewer discussions. The study demonstrates that LLM-generated review feedback can enhance peer review quality by increasing review specificity, actionability, and engagement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its large-scale, randomized controlled trial demonstrating the practical application of LLMs to improve peer review quality *within* the context of an actual conference. Prior works have explored LLM applications for review generation, feedback on manuscripts, or small-scale experiments. This paper goes further by implementing the system at scale and causally demonstrating its positive effects.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant given the growing concerns about declining review quality in AI conferences due to increasing submission volumes. The study offers a practical solution to help alleviate the burden on reviewers and improve the review process.  The demonstration that LLMs can make reviews more actionable and encourage greater engagement between authors and reviewers addresses key pain points in the current review system. The implications extend beyond ICLR to other fields facing similar challenges. However, the impact on acceptance rate was non-significant, showing that even if engagement increases, that doesn't directly translate to a significant rate change.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-scale Randomized Controlled Trial:** Strong experimental design allows for causal inference regarding the impact of LLM feedback.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** Addresses a real-world problem and offers a practical solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** Examines various aspects of review quality and engagement (review length, incorporation rate, rebuttals, score changes, engagement time).
    *   **Reliability Safeguards:** The use of a multi-LLM system and reliability tests strengthens the trustworthiness of the feedback provided to reviewers.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Feedback Content:** The focus on improving *clarity and actionability* of existing reviews might not fundamentally address more substantive issues like identifying methodological flaws or critical analysis of the work's contributions. A more nuanced and holistic approach would add more to the review quality.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Human Editors:** No direct comparison is made with how human review editors might influence review quality, potentially limiting the scope of the results.
    *   **Long-term Effects:** The paper does not address the long-term impact of the system. Could repeated exposure to this feedback improve reviewer writing over time?

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence the design and implementation of future peer review systems, especially within AI and related fields.  It provides concrete evidence that LLMs can be valuable tools for improving the review process. The use of reliability tests is an important lesson for future LLM applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a strong case for the use of LLMs to improve aspects of peer review, leveraging a rigorous experimental design and demonstrating measurable improvements in review actionability and reviewer engagement. Although it doesn't address all aspects of review quality, and could benefit from longer-term or comparison analyses, it is a significant practical contribution to the field.

### Stochastic generative methods for stable and accurate closure modeling of chaotic dynamical systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09750v1)
- **Authors**: Emily Williams, David Darmofal
- **Abstract**: Traditional deterministic subgrid-scale (SGS) models are often dissipative and unstable, especially in regions of chaotic and turbulent flow. Ongoing work in climate science and ocean modeling motivates the use of stochastic SGS models for chaotic dynamics. Further, developing stochastic generative models of underlying dynamics is a rapidly expanding field. In this work, we aim to incorporate stochastic integration toward closure modeling for chaotic dynamical systems. Further, we want to explore the potential stabilizing effect that stochastic models could have on linearized chaotic systems. We propose parametric and generative approaches for closure modeling using stochastic differential equations (SDEs). We derive and implement a quadratic diffusion model based on the fluctuations, demonstrating increased accuracy from bridging theoretical models with generative approaches. Results are demonstrated on the Lorenz-63 dynamical system.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of stochastic generative methods for subgrid-scale (SGS) modeling in chaotic dynamical systems, specifically the Lorenz-63 system. It addresses the limitations of traditional deterministic SGS models, which are often dissipative and unstable. The authors propose using stochastic differential equations (SDEs) and generative models (specifically diffusion models and flow matching) to learn probabilistic distributions of SGS terms.  The paper presents three main approaches: 1) a parametric stochastic model trained via a ResNet to model SGS terms, 2) a model stabilizing linearized chaotic systems with a learned stochastic forcing, and 3) guided diffusion models (with both flow matching and score matching) to sample subgrid stresses conditioned on filtered states, further incorporating a theoretically derived analytical quadratic model. The effectiveness of these methods is demonstrated through increased accuracy and stability compared to deterministic approaches like forward Euler integration, as measured by Wasserstein distance and Hellinger distance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic exploration of generative models, especially diffusion models and score matching, for SGS closure modeling. Applying these relatively recent generative techniques to this problem is a fresh approach. While prior work has explored stochastic SGS models, this paper provides a comparative framework that incorporates theory-informed analytical models with data-driven generative methods.  The combination of parametric models, SDEs for stabilization, and guided diffusion for fluctuation modeling represents a multi-faceted approach that hasn't been extensively explored.

**Significance:**  The potential impact of this work is significant. Stable and accurate SGS models are crucial for efficient and reliable simulations of complex systems like turbulent flows and climate models. By demonstrating improved accuracy and stability, the paper contributes to the ongoing efforts to develop more robust reduced-order models. The use of generative models addresses a key limitation of deterministic models – their inability to capture the full variability of unresolved scales.  Furthermore, the investigation of stochastic models to stabilize linearized chaotic systems could have broader implications beyond closure modeling.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper explores multiple stochastic modeling techniques (parametric models, diffusion models, score matching, analytically derived model) providing a useful comparison.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper describes the methods in sufficient detail, including the architecture of the neural networks and the training procedures.
*   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The use of Wasserstein and Hellinger distances provides a quantitative assessment of the accuracy of the models.
*   **Problem Relevance:** The study addresses a critical problem in computational science and engineering – the development of accurate and stable SGS models.
* **Incorporation of theoretical constraints**: Integrating prior knowledge into the generative models, like in the analytical diffusion model, significantly enhances the overall approach and addresses potential issues regarding physics-consistency of purely data-driven models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses solely on the Lorenz-63 system, a relatively simple chaotic system. While it serves as a proof-of-concept, the scalability and applicability of these methods to higher-dimensional and more complex systems (e.g., turbulent flow) need to be investigated.
*   **Computational Cost:**  The paper doesn't explicitly address the computational cost associated with training and deploying these generative models. Diffusion models are known to be computationally intensive, and the overhead might be a concern for real-world applications. The paper lacks a comprehensive comparative analysis of the additional cost.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** Generative models are often sensitive to hyperparameter tuning. The paper doesn't extensively discuss the sensitivity of the results to different choices of hyperparameters. It should be clear how robust results are.
*   **Interpretability:**  While the paper demonstrates improved accuracy, the interpretability of the learned SGS models remains limited. It would be useful to gain insights into what the models have learned about the underlying dynamics. What are the distributions modeling, and how are they different under various conditions? This is not clear from this paper.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a promising approach to SGS modeling using generative models. While the scope is limited to the Lorenz-63 system, the systematic exploration of different stochastic techniques and the demonstrated improvements in accuracy and stability justify a favorable assessment. The paper opens avenues for future research and development in the field of reduced-order modeling.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates significant potential by incorporating generative and stochastic approaches into a classical modeling framework. The paper does this in a systematic way and provides clear results showing that accuracy is improved. The theoretical justification for integration and the demonstration that stability can be maintained in a chaotic model provide a strong argument. However, the primary weakness is that the paper applies these techniques to a relatively simplified model, the Lorenz-63 system. The absence of a scalability argument and the neglect of any consideration of the computational cost holds the paper back from a higher score. Were it applied to a more complex system, or the discussion considered limitations, it could earn a score closer to 9.

- **Classification**: math.NA
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Multilingual Capabilities with Cultural and Local Knowledge in Large Language Models While Enhancing Native Performance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09753v1)
- **Authors**: Ram Mohan Rao Kadiyala, Siddartha Pullakhandam, Siddhant Gupta, Drishti Sharma, Jebish Purbey, Kanwal Mehreen, Muhammad Arham, Hamza Farooq
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities, but their development has primarily focused on English and other high-resource languages, leaving many languages underserved. We present our latest Hindi-English bi-lingual LLM \textbf{Mantra-14B} with ~3\% average improvement in benchmark scores over both languages, outperforming models twice its size. Using a curated dataset composed of English and Hindi instruction data of 485K samples, we instruction tuned models such as Qwen-2.5-14B-Instruct and Phi-4 to improve performance over both English and Hindi. Our experiments encompassing seven different LLMs of varying parameter sizes and over 140 training attempts with varying English-Hindi training data ratios demonstrated that it is possible to significantly improve multilingual performance without compromising native performance. Further, our approach avoids resource-intensive techniques like vocabulary expansion or architectural modifications, thus keeping the model size small. Our results indicate that modest fine-tuning with culturally and locally informed data can bridge performance gaps without incurring significant computational overhead. We release our training code, datasets, and models under mit and apache licenses to aid further research towards under-represented and low-resource languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents Mantra-14B, a Hindi-English bilingual large language model (LLM). The authors focus on improving multilingual capabilities, specifically Hindi performance, without sacrificing English performance and while maintaining a relatively small model size.  They achieve this through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) on a curated dataset of English and Hindi instruction data. Their results indicate improved performance on both languages compared to the base models (Qwen-2.5-14B-Instruct and Phi-4), and they show it's possible to enhance low-resource language capabilities without substantial architectural changes or computational overhead, offering an alternative to vocabulary expansion or adding layers. They release their training code, datasets, and models under permissive licenses to encourage further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Approach:** The paper tackles a significant problem: the under-representation of low-resource languages in LLM development. Their approach of efficient fine-tuning is computationally accessible, which is crucial for democratizing LLM development.
    *   **Controlled Experiments:**  The paper includes a comprehensive set of experiments with different LLMs, various parameter sizes, and various ratios of English-Hindi training data. The authors performed over 140 fine-tuning attempts.
    *   **Performance Improvement:** The results demonstrate a measurable improvement in Hindi performance while retaining or even slightly improving English performance. The 3% improvement reported is modest but meaningful given the goal of balanced performance and the avoidance of resource-intensive methods. Mantra-14B is even shown to outperform models twice its size in certain benchmarks.
    *   **Reproducibility and Openness:** Releasing the code, datasets, and models under open licenses promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research and development, allowing other researchers to build upon their work.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The domain-wise performance analysis (e.g., MMLU-Pro) and the analysis of model choices provide valuable insights into model behavior. The initial evaluation phase is also well documented.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The techniques used (SFT, LoRA) are well-established in the LLM literature. The novelty lies primarily in the specific application of these techniques to improve Hindi performance and the curated dataset. However, such an improvement using existing techniques can be viewed as an expected, albeit useful, outcome.
    *   **Modest Gains:** While positive, the performance improvements (e.g., a 3% average increase) aren't groundbreaking. Given the substantial computational resources required for LLM training, the practical significance of these incremental gains needs careful consideration in real-world applications. It might not be worth the training cost for some applications.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While the curated dataset is significant (485k samples), compared to datasets used for training the base models or other large multilingual LLMs, it is relatively modest. This limits the potential for more substantial performance gains.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation focuses primarily on standard benchmarks. A more in-depth evaluation of real-world applications (e.g., translation, summarization, content generation) would strengthen the paper. Analysis of toxicity/bias specifically within the Hindi language would add value.
    *   **Limited Comparison to State-of-the-Art Models:** While they compare to existing Hindi LLMs, a more comprehensive benchmark against the absolute state-of-the-art multilingual LLMs would provide a clearer picture of Mantra-14B's relative performance. The comparison mostly focused on efficiency and model size.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper is significant because it provides a practical and accessible approach to improving LLM performance for low-resource languages. The emphasis on computational efficiency and open resources makes this work valuable for researchers and practitioners who may not have access to extensive computational infrastructure. The method's focus on retaining performance in the source language (English) is valuable, highlighting a potential pitfall avoided by this work. The carefully constructed dataset is also a valuable contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6/10**. While the paper addresses an important problem and provides useful insights, the novelty is limited by the use of established techniques. The performance gains, although measurable, are incremental. The paper is strong on reproducibility and openness, but it would benefit from a more comprehensive evaluation and comparison with state-of-the-art models. The relatively modest improvements limit the potential impact on the field, but the practicality of the approach and the release of resources warrant a solid score. The paper clearly demonstrates it is possible to fine-tune and improve the performance of an existing model on Hindi, which justifies a score above average. A higher score would require more substantial improvements and a demonstration of greater novelty and real-world application impact.

### Integrating Large Language Models for Automated Structural Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09754v1)
- **Authors**: Haoran Liang, Mohammad Talebi Kalaleh, Qipei Mei
- **Abstract**: Automated analysis for engineering structures offers considerable potential for boosting efficiency by minimizing repetitive tasks. Although AI-driven methods are increasingly common, no systematic framework yet leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) for automatic structural analysis. To address this gap, we propose a novel framework that integrates LLMs with structural analysis software. LLMs serve as the core engine: they parse structural descriptions from text and translate them into executable Python scripts. Moreover, the framework integrates the generative capabilities of LLMs with code-based finite element (FE) tools like OpenSeesPy. It employs domain-specific prompt design and in-context learning strategies to enhance the LLM's problem-solving capabilities and generative stability, enabling fully automated structural analysis from descriptive text to model outputs. In our experiments, we introduce a well-curated small-scale benchmark dataset of 20 structural analysis word problems (SAWPs) with ground-truth solutions and evaluate the performance of different LLMs within our framework in solving these SAWPs. The role of system instructions, crafted by structural engineers, is also investigated to understand their impact on LLM-driven structural analysis. Additionally, the generative stability of our framework is examined. Through multiple validation experiments on the benchmark, our results demonstrate that the proposed framework can substantially increase the level of automation in solving SAWPs compared to traditional methods. Quantitatively, the framework, built on GPT-4o, achieved 100% accuracy, surpassing GPT-4 (85%), Gemini 1.5 Pro (80%), and Llama-3.3 (30%) on the test examples. Furthermore, integrating domain-specific instructions enhanced performance by 30% on problems with asymmetrical structural configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for automating structural analysis by integrating large language models (LLMs) with finite element analysis software (OpenSeesPy).  It addresses the gap in current AI-driven structural analysis by leveraging LLMs to parse structural descriptions from text, translate them into executable Python scripts, and generate visualizations. The proposed framework uses domain-specific prompt design and in-context learning (ICL) to improve LLM's problem-solving abilities and generative stability. The authors create a small-scale benchmark dataset (SAWPs) of 20 structural analysis word problems with ground-truth solutions to evaluate the performance of various LLMs (GPT-4, GPT-4o, Gemini 1.5 Pro, and Llama 3). The impact of system instructions, tailored by structural engineers, is also investigated.  The framework shows a substantial increase in automation, with GPT-4o achieving 100% accuracy on the benchmark. The integration of domain-specific instructions further enhances performance, particularly for asymmetrical structures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for automated structural analysis is relatively novel. While there have been explorations of LLMs in civil engineering, the systematic framework that directly connects natural language descriptions of structural problems to FEA software via LLM-generated code appears to be a significant contribution. The curated SAWPs dataset is also a valuable addition, as it provides a standardized way to evaluate LLM performance in this specific context. However, the types of structural systems addressed (simple 2D frames) are fairly basic, limiting the current impact of the work. Prior work has explored LLM applications in programming for FEA, which slightly tempers the novelty of this direct integration.

**Significance:** The work's significance lies in its potential to streamline the structural analysis process, reduce manual effort, and make FEA more accessible to engineers with less coding experience. By demonstrating that LLMs can interpret structural descriptions and generate correct analysis code, the paper opens up possibilities for more automated design workflows. The detailed exploration of prompting strategies and system instructions is useful for guiding future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem statement and well-defined framework:** The paper clearly articulates the need for automation in structural analysis and presents a detailed framework for achieving this.
*   **Systematic evaluation:** The use of a benchmark dataset and comparative analysis of different LLMs provides a solid basis for evaluating the framework's performance.
*   **Emphasis on practical considerations:** The study addresses issues like generative stability and the impact of system instructions, which are crucial for real-world applications.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The achievement of 100% accuracy with GPT-4o demonstrates the potential of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope of benchmark problems:** The focus on simple 2D frames restricts the generalizability of the findings to more complex structural systems.
*   **Relatively small dataset size:** While the SAWPs dataset is well-curated, a larger dataset would strengthen the statistical significance of the results and better assess the framework's robustness.
*   **Lack of comparison with existing automated FEA methods:** The paper doesn't compare the proposed approach with existing automated FEA workflows or scripting tools, making it difficult to assess its relative advantages. The comparison is essentially against manual work, rather than other automation techniques.
*   **Dependence on closed-source LLMs:** Reliance on GPT-4 and other proprietary LLMs raises concerns about reproducibility and accessibility.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above considerations, the paper warrants a score of **7**.

*   **Reasoning for the score:** The framework is novel in its specific approach to combining LLMs and FEA, shows promising results, and has the potential to impact structural analysis workflows. However, the limited scope, dataset size, absence of comparisons with other automation techniques, and reliance on closed-source models temper the overall impact and broader applicability of the study in its current form. The work represents an important step forward, but further research is needed to address the limitations and fully realize its potential.  Improvements such as addressing more complex structures, benchmarking against alternative tools, and focusing on open-source LLMs would considerably strengthen the work and its potential impact.

### Alleviating the Fear of Losing Alignment in LLM Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09757v1)
- **Authors**: Kang Yang, Guanhong Tao, Xun Chen, Jun Xu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated revolutionary capabilities in understanding complex contexts and performing a wide range of tasks. However, LLMs can also answer questions that are unethical or harmful, raising concerns about their applications. To regulate LLMs' responses to such questions, a training strategy called \textit{alignment} can help. Yet, alignment can be unexpectedly compromised when fine-tuning an LLM for downstream tasks. This paper focuses on recovering the alignment lost during fine-tuning. We observe that there are two distinct directions inherent in an aligned LLM: the \textit{aligned direction} and the \textit{harmful direction}. An LLM is inclined to answer questions in the aligned direction while refusing queries in the harmful direction. Therefore, we propose to recover the harmful direction of the fine-tuned model that has been compromised. Specifically, we restore a small subset of the fine-tuned model's weight parameters from the original aligned model using gradient descent. We also introduce a rollback mechanism to avoid aggressive recovery and maintain downstream task performance. Our evaluation on 125 fine-tuned LLMs demonstrates that our method can reduce their harmful rate (percentage of answering harmful questions) from 33.25\% to 1.74\%, without sacrificing task performance much. In contrast, the existing methods either only reduce the harmful rate to a limited extent or significantly impact the normal functionality. Our code is available at https://github.com/kangyangWHU/LLMAlignment
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of compromised alignment in large language models (LLMs) after fine-tuning.  It observes that aligned LLMs have two inherent directions: an aligned direction and a harmful direction. The paper proposes a method to recover the harmful direction of the fine-tuned model by restoring a small subset of weight parameters from the original aligned model using gradient descent. A rollback mechanism is also introduced to prevent aggressive recovery and maintain downstream task performance. The evaluation on fine-tuned LLMs shows that the method can reduce harmful rate without significantly sacrificing task performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles an important and timely problem. The observation about aligned and harmful directions is a valuable conceptual contribution.  The proposed method, using gradient descent and a rollback mechanism, is a practical approach to re-align fine-tuned models. The experimental results demonstrate that the method can be effective.

However, the paper's main weakness lies in its limited novelty.  The core idea of using gradient descent to adjust model weights is not new, and the rollback mechanism is a fairly standard technique. The approach builds significantly upon prior work in LLM alignment and steering, thus making it a marginal improvement. The strength lies in providing another practical solution and evaluating it across a diverse set of models and tasks. The experiments demonstrate a clear reduction in the harmful rate, but performance gains were not significant across all models.

The method may struggle in more complex scenarios. The reliance on a small recovery dataset could be a limiting factor if the space of harmful prompts is very large or if the harmful directions substantially changed after fine-tuning. How does the performance generalize across tasks.

The paper’s significance is moderate. It confirms recent findings regarding the vulnerability of fine-tuning LLMs. And provides another means to mitigate safety risks.

Overall, the paper offers a practical solution to a relevant problem but its novelty is relatively low and the practical benefits for all types of models and tasks are relatively unconvincing.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**
The score reflects the paper's practical value in re-aligning fine-tuned LLMs and demonstrating this effectiveness experimentally. The downside is its limited novelty and that it builds on existing methods for LLM steering and gradient-based weight adjustment. There's room for improvement in generalizing to more complex scenarios and scaling up to high-dimensional parameter spaces and large corpora of textual training data.
- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Socratic Chart: Cooperating Multiple Agents for Robust SVG Chart Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09764v1)
- **Authors**: Yuyang Ji, Haohan Wang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown remarkable versatility but face challenges in demonstrating true visual understanding, particularly in chart reasoning tasks. Existing benchmarks like ChartQA reveal significant reliance on text-based shortcuts and probabilistic pattern-matching rather than genuine visual reasoning. To rigorously evaluate visual reasoning, we introduce a more challenging test scenario by removing textual labels and introducing chart perturbations in the ChartQA dataset. Under these conditions, models like GPT-4o and Gemini-2.0 Pro experience up to a 30% performance drop, underscoring their limitations. To address these challenges, we propose Socratic Chart, a new framework that transforms chart images into Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) representations, enabling MLLMs to integrate textual and visual modalities for enhanced chart understanding. Socratic Chart employs a multi-agent pipeline with specialized agent-generators to extract primitive chart attributes (e.g., bar heights, line coordinates) and an agent-critic to validate results, ensuring high-fidelity symbolic representations. Our framework surpasses state-of-the-art models in accurately capturing chart primitives and improving reasoning performance, establishing a robust pathway for advancing MLLM visual understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Socratic Chart," a framework designed to improve the visual understanding of charts by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  It addresses the issue that MLLMs often rely on textual shortcuts in charts, rather than genuine visual reasoning. Socratic Chart converts chart images into Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) representations, which provides a structured, semantic description of the chart's elements.  A multi-agent pipeline is employed, with specialized agents extracting chart attributes (e.g., bar heights, line coordinates) and an agent-critic validating the results.  The SVG representation, along with the original image and query, are then fed to the MLLM.  Evaluations on ChartQA and related benchmarks demonstrate that Socratic Chart outperforms existing models like GPT-4V and Gemini-2.0 Pro, especially in scenarios where textual labels are removed or charts are perturbed. The paper also demonstrates how the proposed approach is robust in handling distorted charts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using SVG representations to provide MLLMs with a structured chart description has novelty.  While using vector graphics isn't new in itself, the application of this to improve *reasoning* specifically in MLLMs, combined with the multi-agent architecture, presents a novel approach. The experimental setup of removing labels and perturbing charts to specifically test "true" visual reasoning is also a valuable contribution.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical weakness of current MLLMs—their over-reliance on textual cues in visual tasks.  If MLLMs are to truly understand visual data, they must be able to reason about it directly. By demonstrating how Socratic Chart can mitigate this issue, the paper makes a significant step towards more robust and reliable chart understanding. The ablation studies are also important, as they provide insight on the contributions of the individual components.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of MLLMs in chart understanding and motivates the need for a solution that focuses on visual reasoning.
    * **Well-Defined Method:** The Socratic Chart framework is well-described, and the multi-agent architecture provides a clear structure for extracting and validating chart information.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The results on ChartQA and its modified variants (label removal, perturbation) convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The fact that the system has robust performance for distorted data is also important.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of the different components of the Socratic Chart framework.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Overhead:** The paper acknowledges the computational overhead of the multi-agent pipeline.  While increased accuracy is valuable, the practical implications of this overhead need to be carefully considered, especially for real-time applications. More details on the runtime performance would be helpful.
    * **Dependency on MLLM:**  The framework is ultimately dependent on the capabilities of the underlying MLLM.  While it addresses a key issue in MLLMs, it might not be directly transferable to models with fundamentally different architectures or reasoning mechanisms.
    * **Limited Generalization?** While the paper tests on Charixv, which includes more complex chart types, it might be beneficial to evaluate performance with data from a more expansive dataset of visualizations.
    * **Minor Limitations:** While the method outperforms SOTA approaches in most cases, there are a few examples where the performance is only comparable or lower.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a compelling solution to a real problem in MLLM-based chart understanding.  The novelty of the approach, combined with the strong experimental results and insights from the ablation studies, make this a significant contribution to the field. However, the computational overhead, dependency on the underlying MLLM and some dataset-specific limitations need to be considered.

### Two Heads are Better Than One: Test-time Scaling of Multi-agent Collaborative Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09772v1)
- **Authors**: Can Jin, Hongwu Peng, Qixin Zhang, Yujin Tang, Dimitris N. Metaxas, Tong Che
- **Abstract**: Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) offer a promising path toward solving complex, real-world tasks that single-agent systems often struggle to manage. While recent advancements in test-time scaling (TTS) have significantly improved single-agent performance on challenging reasoning tasks, how to effectively scale collaboration and reasoning in MAS remains an open question. In this work, we introduce an adaptive multi-agent framework designed to enhance collaborative reasoning through both model-level training and system-level coordination. We construct M500, a high-quality dataset containing 500 multi-agent collaborative reasoning traces, and fine-tune Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct on this dataset to produce M1-32B, a model optimized for multi-agent collaboration. To further enable adaptive reasoning, we propose a novel CEO agent that dynamically manages the discussion process, guiding agent collaboration and adjusting reasoning depth for more effective problem-solving. Evaluated in an open-source MAS across a range of tasks-including general understanding, mathematical reasoning, and coding-our system significantly outperforms strong baselines. For instance, M1-32B achieves 12% improvement on GPQA-Diamond, 41% on AIME2024, and 10% on MBPP-Sanitized, matching the performance of state-of-the-art models like DeepSeek-R1 on some tasks. These results highlight the importance of both learned collaboration and adaptive coordination in scaling multi-agent reasoning. Code is available at https://github.com/jincan333/MAS-TTS
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Two Heads are Better Than One: Test-time Scaling of Multi-agent Collaborative Reasoning" addresses the challenge of scaling collaborative reasoning in Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) powered by Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors introduce an adaptive MAS framework designed to enhance collaboration through both model-level training (fine-tuning) and system-level coordination.  A key contribution is the creation of M500, a high-quality dataset of multi-agent collaborative reasoning traces, used to fine-tune Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, resulting in the M1-32B model. Furthermore, the paper presents a "CEO" agent that dynamically manages the discussion process within the MAS, guiding agent collaboration and adjusting reasoning depth. The system's performance is evaluated across general understanding, mathematical reasoning, and coding tasks, demonstrating significant improvements over strong baselines and achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art models like DeepSeek-R1 on certain tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits moderate novelty. While MAS and test-time scaling (TTS) are established areas, the specific combination of:
    *   Fine-tuning for collaborative reasoning using the M500 dataset.
    *   Introducing a CEO agent for adaptive management of collaboration.
    *   Evaluating this combination across a diverse set of reasoning and coding tasks in an open-source MAS framework is a novel approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper has notable significance, especially in the context of scaling LLMs for complex tasks. The key highlights are:

    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The M500 dataset, if indeed high-quality as claimed, represents a valuable resource for the community to research and develop MAS for reasoning tasks. The detail concerning how the dataset is curated and filtered helps increase its impact.
    *   **Adaptive Coordination:** The CEO agent represents an important step towards effectively managing MAS reasoning, addressing the challenges of resource allocation and guiding discussions for complex tasks.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The significant performance improvements achieved by M1-32B with the CEO agent across different tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach and point toward a promising research direction. However, the performance is not always superior compared to the SOTA, so these gains are not decisive.
    *   **Limitations:** The paper has a few limitations that constrain the score.
        *   The reliance on Qwen2.5 as a base model means the results could potentially be further improved with stronger base LLMs.
        *   The comparison could also benefit from additional ablation studies. It would be interesting to see results using other model sizes.
        *   The paper assumes that the improvement compared to S1.1-32B can be attributed to the data's nature, but there could be other factors at play (such as differences in architecture or training settings).

*   **Impact:**  The research has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and robust MAS for problem-solving, particularly in domains requiring complex reasoning and collaboration. The open-source availability of the code enhances its potential impact.

*   **Rigour:** The paper exhibits good rigour in terms of its experiments and the evaluation of results. The datasets used in the experiment are well established and provide fair comparison to other SOTA LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's contributions lie in combining existing concepts (MAS, TTS, fine-tuning) in a novel way, creating a valuable dataset, and demonstrating significant performance gains through an adaptive coordination mechanism. The significance is further enhanced by the availability of the code, promoting future research. The novelty is moderate, but the significant performance boosts are valuable. Based on these strengths and the points listed above, the paper makes a worthwhile contribution to the field.

### Understanding and Optimizing Multi-Stage AI Inference Pipelines
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09775v1)
- **Authors**: Abhimanyu Rajeshkumar Bambhaniya, Hanjiang Wu, Suvinay Subramanian, Sudarshan Srinivasan, Souvik Kundu, Amir Yazdanbakhsh, Midhilesh Elavazhagan, Madhu Kumar, Tushar Krishna
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of Large Language Models (LLMs) has driven the need for increasingly sophisticated inference pipelines and hardware platforms. Modern LLM serving extends beyond traditional prefill-decode workflows, incorporating multi-stage processes such as Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), key-value (KV) cache retrieval, dynamic model routing, and multi step reasoning. These stages exhibit diverse computational demands, requiring distributed systems that integrate GPUs, ASICs, CPUs, and memory-centric architectures. However, existing simulators lack the fidelity to model these heterogeneous, multi-engine workflows, limiting their ability to inform architectural decisions. To address this gap, we introduce HERMES, a Heterogeneous Multi-stage LLM inference Execution Simulator. HERMES models diverse request stages; including RAG, KV retrieval, reasoning, prefill, and decode across complex hardware hierarchies. HERMES supports heterogeneous clients executing multiple models concurrently unlike prior frameworks while incorporating advanced batching strategies and multi-level memory hierarchies. By integrating real hardware traces with analytical modeling, HERMES captures critical trade-offs such as memory bandwidth contention, inter-cluster communication latency, and batching efficiency in hybrid CPU-accelerator deployments. Through case studies, we explore the impact of reasoning stages on end-to-end latency, optimal batching strategies for hybrid pipelines, and the architectural implications of remote KV cache retrieval. HERMES empowers system designers to navigate the evolving landscape of LLM inference, providing actionable insights into optimizing hardware-software co-design for next-generation AI workloads.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Understanding and Optimizing Multi-Stage AI Inference Pipelines":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HERMES, a new simulator designed to model and optimize the complex inference pipelines of modern Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing simulators that are primarily focused on the traditional prefill-decode stages and are unable to accurately model the diverse computational demands and hardware heterogeneity of contemporary LLM serving systems. HERMES models multi-stage workflows like Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), KV cache retrieval, dynamic model routing, and multi-step reasoning, across heterogeneous hardware including GPUs, ASICs, CPUs, and memory-centric architectures. It integrates real hardware traces with analytical modeling to capture crucial trade-offs such as memory bandwidth contention, inter-cluster communication latency, and batching efficiency. Case studies explore the impact of reasoning stages, optimal batching strategies, and the architectural implications of remote KV cache retrieval.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by tackling a significant and increasingly important problem in the LLM field: the efficient simulation and optimization of complex, multi-stage inference pipelines. Existing simulators have struggled to keep pace with the rapid evolution of LLM serving, leaving a gap in the tools available for hardware-software co-design.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a critical need:** The simulator targets a genuine pain point in the LLM community.  As LLMs become more sophisticated, their inference workflows become more complex, requiring tools that can accurately model and optimize these processes.
*   **Comprehensive Modeling:** The simulator's ability to model diverse request stages (RAG, KV retrieval, reasoning, prefill, decode) and heterogeneous hardware configurations is a significant step forward.  The integration of multi-level memory hierarchies and advanced batching strategies provides a more realistic representation of modern serving systems.
*   **Integration of real-world traces:** The use of real hardware traces coupled with analytical modeling improves the accuracy and fidelity of the simulation results.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The case studies provide concrete examples of how HERMES can be used to inform architectural decisions, such as optimizing batching strategies for hybrid pipelines and understanding the impact of remote KV cache retrieval.
*   **Modular design:** The modular design of HERMES allows for seamless integration of existing hardware simulators, which further improves accuracy.
*   **End-to-End Simulation of SOTA LLM inference pipelines:** The simulator supports end-to-end execution of SOTA LLM inference pipelines over diverse hardware clients(GPU,CPU,TPU, etc).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Validation challenges:**  While the paper demonstrates fidelity by comparing HERMES's predictions to existing frameworks (Splitwise), it acknowledges the difficulty in validating against real large-scale systems. The lack of access to those systems can impact the paper's claims. The system has a limited validation on end to end runtime against hardware traces or existing systems such as vLLM.
*   **Model complexity:** Although the paper mentions utilizing ML modeling for faster simulation, the use of ML prediction in this manner can potentially introduce approximation errors. The choice of ML models is not justified,
*   **Scalability:** While mentioned, the paper does not provide a very detail analysis for scalability.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The primary novelty lies in HERMES's ability to simulate heterogeneous, multi-stage LLM inference pipelines with a level of fidelity not found in existing simulators. This allows for a more comprehensive understanding of the complex interactions between different hardware components, software strategies, and request types. This capability is significant because it can enable system designers to make more informed decisions about hardware-software co-design, leading to more efficient and cost-effective LLM serving systems. The simulator addresses complex challenges within the LLM domain for architecture designers.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and useful tool that addresses a significant gap in the LLM serving ecosystem. While the limitations in large-scale validation and potential error from the choice of ML model are drawbacks, the comprehensive modeling capabilities, integration of real hardware traces, actionable insights, and modular design make HERMES a valuable contribution. The ability to simulate heterogeneous systems, the inclusion of diverse request stages, and the support for advanced batching strategies all contribute to the paper's practical significance.  HERMES has the potential to influence the design of next-generation LLM serving systems by providing a more accurate and detailed understanding of the complex interactions within these systems.

**Score: 7.5**
